News

Market analysis

US distillate stocks seen lower amid strong exports: survey
Inventories see third weekly draw, fall 1.5 mil barrels
Exports surge to 1.8 million b/d in October
Refinery utilization falls to 92.2% capacity
US distillate fuel oil stocks likely fell to six-week lows during the week to Oct. 2, analysts surveyed by Platts said Oct. 5, against a backdrop of strong exports and easing refinery runs. Platts is part of S&P Global Energy.
Nationwide distillate stocks likely declined 1.5 million barrels to around 103.7 million barrels during the period, analysts said. The third straight weekly draw would put stockpiles at 12.5% behind the seasonal five-year average of US Energy Information Administration data and leave them at the lowest outright level since mid-August.
US diesel and gasoil exports have averaged 1.8 million barrels/ day to date in October, data from S&P Global Commodities at Sea showed, up from 1.5 million b/d during September. At that rate October 2026 exports would be in the running for all-time high.
Arbitrage economics for US diesel exports to Europe, which have averaged around 380,000 b/d in October, came under pressure last week after the G7 said on Oct. 2 that its members would release 100 million barrels of oil stocks over the next four months. The decision included a commitment for the G7 and partner countries to frontload a "substantial diesel release" in the next 20 days, with members to discuss the possibility of additional releases in coordination with the IEA.
Following this announcement, US President Donald Trump told reporters that he was ruling out a potential diesel export ban
(continued on page 22)

International Crude
Saudi Aramco lowers Asia Nov crude OSP differentials by $3-$5/b MOM
Asia-bound Arab Light cut by $3/b from Oct US, Europe-bound cargoes steady to $3/b higher MOM

Saudi Aramco lowered the November official selling price differentials for its crude grades bound for Asia by $3-$5/ barrel month over month, according to a notice from the producer Oct. 5.
Aramco set the November OSP differential for its flagship Arab Light bound for Asia at a $5/b discount to the Oman/Dubai average, down $3/b month over month, the notice said.
The November OSP differentials for Asia-bound Extra Light and Super Light were also lowered by $3/b, while those for the Medium and Heavy grades were lowered by $5/b, according to the notice.
The November OSP differentials for crude cargoes bound for Europe and the Mediterranean were all raised by $3/b month over month, while those for US-bound cargoes were kept steady over the same period, the company said.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 5, 2026

Aramco says replenishing oil stocks while meeting demand to take up to 2 years
Meeting all long-term customers' requirements
Pressure on both ends of the barrel to intensify
Oil infrastructure intact despite attacks
Saudi Arabian Oil Co. CEO Amin Nasser said Oct. 5 that replenishing oil inventories while meeting demand could take up to two years, as the world grapples with a major supply crisis as a result of the conflict in the Middle East.
Speaking at the Energy Intelligence Forum in London, Nasser said that existing buffers, including releasing inventories, have cushioned the blow to supply, but that they are finite and emergency reserves cannot fix long-term supply issues.
"Most of that came from onshore commercial inventories, which was the last major tool in the box," he said.
"If this does not normalize soon we'll find out we're building a much bigger hole in terms of inventories reaching a stress level," he said.
Aramco estimates that less than 10% of inventory is available, with most of it pipeline fill, minimum tank levels and needed for operations.

"Until it fully reopens, I'm confident that the crude reality is that pressure... will intensify," Nasser said. "While the squeeze on crude is serious, refined fuel prices have risen even more sharply," he added.
Nasser said that physical barrels cost much more than the price reflected in benchmarks.
"Sometimes $20-30/b more. The whole thing is masked by inventories," he said.
Aramco expects global demand to be resilient because the crisis is a supply disruption, not a demand disruption.
"Globally I think 2 million b/d of additional demand will come over 18-24 months, demand will be resilient going forward," he said.
Aramco expects Chinese demand to continue to increase, with demand from liquids-to-chemicals processing seen growing by 3 million b/d.
-- Rosemary Griffin
Congolese crude Djeno falls to $21/b discount to Dated Brent, a record low
Lowest assessment since launch in August 2013
Freight rates, weak Chinese demand pressure differentials

Attacks Saudi Arabia has incurred major disruptions to its energy
operations since the US and Israel attacked Iran Feb. 28, but Nasser said that infrastructure is intact and the company is restoring operations within a matter of days following attacks -- a restoration rate that is "six times faster" than anywhere else in the world.
"We have multiple systems that allow us to continue to supply the markets and always during this crisis we were able to meet crude sales agreements around the world, to Asia and Europe," Nasser said.
He pointed out that pipelines include multiple lines with a lot of redundancy and flexibility built in.
Aramco continues to export via Yanbu on the Red Sea, through the Mediterranean via Sidi Kerir and Port Said, as well as through the Strait of Hormuz, Nasser said.
"We are capitalizing on all export routes, we use a lot of STS [ship-to-ship transfers]; we will take it outside to a safe place. We cater for our customers' requirements," he said.
He said that additional export routes are also being worked on, with feasibility and engineering works underway. "At Aramco, we are studying additional routes for crude export while exploring more overseas storage to help cover short-term disruptions," he said, without giving details.
Nasser said that shareholders are supportive of the need to invest in increasing resilience in the system.
Price pressure Oil prices have risen significantly during the conflict in the
Middle East. Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed Dated Brent at $126.955/barrel on Oct.2, up from around $70/b before the war broke out.

Djeno crude from the Republic of the Congo was assessed by Platts at an all-time low of a $21/barrel discount to Dated Brent on Oct. 5 as traders said shipping economics had eroded the grade's competitiveness into its key outlet in Asia.
The Oct. 5 free-on-board assessment of Djeno -- meaning buyers bear the cost of tanker chartering -- fell by $5.40/b day over day, and has softened by more than $14/b across the past week. A $21/b discount represents the softest value since Platts' assessment of the grade was launched in August 2013.
TotalEnergies offered two separate cargoes of Djeno in the day's Platts Market on Close assessment process, loading respectively across Nov. 10-11 and Nov. 19-20, with both offers withdrawn prior to the London close at $16.05/barrel and $15.35/b discounts to Dated Brent, without enticing a buyer.
Three separate market sources told Platts on the day that offers in the wider WAF market for such cargoes were as low as a $20/b discount with no interest shown. Two of the sources suggested that value would more likely be found closer to a $30/b discount to Dated Brent.
"I don't think these kinds of costs and changes in costs have ever been seen before. The big freight numbers, big scale of costs and uncertainty of those costs day-to-day must be making it extremely hard for buyers and sellers to come to an agreement on crude value," a West African crude trader told Platts on Oct. 5.
While elevated freight rates were doing the heavy lifting in the bearish price movements, a second trader said Djeno's key demand center in China was "a lost hope, right now" with its usual pull all but absent.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- George Delaney

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 5, 2026

Russia September crude exports rise 8% on month, despite fresh sanctions
Crude exports to unknown destinations surge to 709,000 b/d
Crude deliveries to India plunge 59% on month
Sept product exports fall 11% on month
Russia exported 4.088 million barrels/day of seaborne crude in September, up 8% month over month and up 3% year over year, despite ongoing sanctions, ship tracking data from S&P Global Energy showed Oct. 5.
US President Donald Trump signed legislation on Sept. 18 allowing him to impose tariffs of up to 100% on goods imported from the five largest importers of Russian crude oil or gas by volume, and to impose sanctions on shadow fleet vessels used to evade sanctions.
Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov said Sept. 21 that it "does not help in reviving our bilateral relations, nor is it likely to facilitate successful progress toward a settlement in Ukraine," in a call with reporters broadcast on the Kremlin's telegram channel.
India and China are the biggest buyers of Russian crude. The Indian foreign ministry said it will monitor the situation and is committed to ensuring that the country's energy requirements are met. The Chinese ministry of commerce said Sept. 19 that it opposes unilateral sanctions that lack a basis in international law as well as secondary sanctions.
Russian seaborne crude exports to India in September dropped 59% month over month and 51% year over year to 816,000 b/d, while exports to China climbed 22% month over

month and 27% year over year to 1.415 million b/d, according to data from S&P Global Commodities at Sea.
Exports to unknown destinations surged to 709,000 b/d in September, up from 24,000 b/d in August and up from zero a year earlier, CAS data showed. Exports of Russian crude bound for ship-to-ship transfer operations in the Far East dropped 43% month over month and 56% year over year to 142,000 b/d, the data showed.
Russian Economy Minister Maxim Reshetnikov said Sept. 24 that Russia will produce 494 million metric tons of oil in 2026, according to a government statement. This is equivalent to about 9.92 million b/d and would be a drop of around 3.5% from 2025 levels of 512 million mt.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed Russian Urals crude on a FOB basis at Primorsk at $91.85/b Oct. 2, a discount of $35.11/b to Dated Brent. The discount averaged $29.55/b in September, against a five-year average of $20.79/b.

Diesel ban Russia decided to extend a full diesel export ban until the end
of October, according to Russian newspaper Vedomosti, citing two sources familiar with the matter.
Restrictions on producers have been extended to ensure fuel supply to the domestic market, Vedomosti reported.
The ban for non-producers will remain in place until the end of January 2027. The same deadline applies to all gasoline exports, according to the Russian government decree.
Russia's seaborne exports of refined products in September fell 11% month over month and 42% year over year to 1.075 million b/d, amid continued attacks on its refining infrastructure.
Exports to China dropped 56% month over month and 49% year over year to 109,829 b/d, while exports to Egypt grew 18% month over month and 31% year over year to 150,775 b/d, CAS data showed. Egypt is a key transshipment hub for Russian barrels.
Russian presidential envoy for investment and economic cooperation Kirill Dmitriev said that US diesel prices will rise as a result of US sanctions legislation.
"US Congress sanction threats and [Ukrainian President Volodymyr] Zelenskyy's attacks are affecting global energy markets and driving US diesel prices to a projected $8+ a gallon on election day," he said in a post on social media platform X Sept. 20.
-- Thomas Washington
Australia forecasts 15% rise in crude imports as exports slow, field output declines
Domestic oil field output to rise before falling
Middle East conflict threatens diesel supply
Brent crude may rise to $135/b
Australia expects crude oil and condensate imports to rise 14.5% in the fiscal year 2026-27, while exports are forecast to fall 7.9%, according to a government outlook released Oct. 2 that also warned that prolonged disruption in the Middle East could tighten diesel supplies.
Imports are forecast to reach about 213,000 barrels/day in the fiscal year ending June 2027, up from 186,000 b/d in 202526, while exports are projected to decline to 227,000 b/d from 247,000 b/d, according to the September Resources and Energy Quarterly released by the Office of the Chief Economist.
The projections indicate lower crude oil and condensate exports as output from mature Australian fields declines, while the country remains reliant on imported fuels. The outlook assumes that Middle Eastern trade flows normalize before fuel markets come under greater supply pressure.
Australia's crude and condensate exports are projected to fall to 213,000 b/d in FY 2027-28 and 182,000 b/d by FY 2030-31, about 26% below FY 2025-26 levels, according to the government outlook.
Imports are forecast to remain near 213,000 b/d from FY 2026-27 onward, exceeding exports from FY 2028-29, the outlook showed.

Domestic crude and condensate production is expected to rise by 2.9% to 233,000 b/d in FY 2026-27, before declining to 221,000 b/d in FY 2027-28 and 194,000 b/d in FY 2030-31, according to the outlook.
Given Australia's much larger finished-fuel import market, government data showed that refined-product imports rose 5% to 54.4 billion liters in 2025-26, equivalent to about 937,000 b/d. Import value increased 25.2% to A$56.6 billion, while domestic refinery product output remained broadly steady at 14.2 billion liters.
The government said damage to Russian refineries, constrained Middle Eastern refining capacity and lower Chinese refinery runs disproportionately lifted refined-product prices while easing pressure on crude markets.
Continued conflict would have an "outsized impact on product markets (particularly diesel)," the government said.
Inventory depletion "If trade continues to be disrupted until inventories begin to
reach very low levels, it is expected that Brent crude oil prices will begin to rise sharply," the government said. "Prices could average US$135/barrel in the December quarter," it said.
The outlook noted that low inventories could cause localized shortages of oil products and disrupt supply chains, as pipelines and storage facilities require minimum operating volumes. Governments could increase purchases from international markets to protect energy security, adding pressure on prices, according to the outlook.
Brent could average $135/b in the fourth quarter of 2026 under this prolonged disruption scenario, compared with $101.11/b in the baseline scenario, the outlook showed.
The government's baseline outlook forecasts Brent falling to $77.50/b in the first quarter of 2027 and $69.58/b in the fourth quarter as transits through the Strait of Hormuz and the Bab al-Mandab Strait normalize. Annual Brent prices are forecast at $95.7/b in 2026, $73.6/b in 2027, and $67/b in 2028, in nominal terms, according to the outlook.
"Once trade resumes through the Strait of Hormuz, prices are expected to decline over a period of several years as production comes back online much more slowly than in the central (baseline) scenario," the chief economist's office said.
The scenarios are benchmarked to Platts Dated Brent -- the global reference price for physical North Sea crude loading 10 days to one month forward -- which was last assessed at $126.955/b on Oct. 2.

Fundamentals Global oil supply is forecast to recover to 108.3 million b/d in
2027 from 100.6 million b/d in 2026, exceeding projected demand of 105.2 million b/d; returning Middle Eastern volumes and supply growth from Brazil, Guyana and Canada are expected to underpin the recovery, according to the outlook.
"Middle Eastern producers are expected to restore production to preconflict levels or higher once transit through the Strait of Hormuz and the Bab al-Mandab Strait resumes," the chief economist's office said. "The additional supply is likely to push the global market into surplus ... Production is then expected to remain near this level for several years, easing slightly in 2031 as fields mature."
However, replenishing depleted inventories is expected to keep prices above levels otherwise implied by the projected surplus, according to the outlook.
Australian crude and condensate export earnings are forecast at A$10.4 billion in FY 2026-27, revised up by A$700 million from the June outlook because of higher conflict-related prices, the government said. Nominal earnings are projected to fall to A$7.7 billion in FY 2027-28 and A$6.2 billion by FY 2030-31, according to the government.
-- Mia Pei
Kuwaiti oil production back to around 2 mil b/d
Global buffers to mitigate supply shortages `evaporating'
Plans to expand own strategic tanker fleet
6 mil b/d of Gulf refined products left the market
Kuwait has increased oil output to around 2 million barrels/day, Kuwait Petroleum Corp. CEO Shaikh Nawaf S. Al-Sabah said Oct. 5, after production fell earlier in the year as a result of attacks and shipping restrictions caused by the war in the Middle East.
Kuwait initially cut output to levels required only for domestic consumption, he said, as it was unable to export any volumes, but is now exporting using its own strategic tanker fleet.
"And we have the capacity to go back up to full production and even to our current maximum sustainable capacity of 3 million b/d if we have the export routes available," he said.
Kuwait was producing around 2.6 million b/d before the US and Israel attacked Iran on Feb. 28.
"We learned from the Iraqi invasion and subsequent war in 2003 that we needed both strategic storage outside the Strait [of Hormuz] and a larger fleet that can run shuttle services outside the Gulf," he said.
KPC is in talks with international partners to expand this fleet, either through ownership or various leasing options, he said.
In addition to ship-to-ship transfers, some KPC customers are now bringing tankers into the Gulf, and more will follow, he said.
Buffers `evaporating' Al-Sabah said that alternative routes and strategic storage
have acted as a buffer but cannot replace free navigation via the Strait of Hormuz.

"The world has been using these buffers, but these buffers are quickly evaporating -- whether it's [strategic petroleum reserves] coming down to incredibly low levels, whether it is strategic storage that we have put in countries around the world, also being moved up very quickly," he said. "And ultimately, the resilience of the international system can only hold up for so long."
Concerns are growing around refined product supply shortages and elevated prices, with the US mulling a diesel export ban and restrictions already in place in Russia.
KPC estimates that around 6 million b/d of refined products previously produced in the Gulf have left the market. Before the war, Kuwait was Europe's biggest jet fuel supplier and the second-largest diesel supplier.
"There's just simply not enough refining capacity in the world to make up for shuttered capacity in the Gulf," he said.
"The pipelines that Saudi Arabia and the UAE have are important and strategic, but they deliver crude," he said. "We have a lot of VLCCs in our tanker fleet... we have some MRs as well that deliver product. But what you're seeing on the international market right now is an abundance of crude on the market but not product."
"So moving more LRs, more MRs, I think is really the name of the game in this next period to try to move product out of the Gulf to get the refineries at full production," he said.
KPC is in talks with Saudi Arabia and the UAE on pipeline and port options and is looking to increase domestic storage and stocks at its overseas refineries, including in Japan and South Korea.
The company is also looking for foreign investment in projects, including pipelines and offshore fields. It has announced three offshore discoveries and Al-Sabah said that subsequent drilling has revealed further discoveries.
He called on consumers and producers alike to recognize that the situation requires a strategic response rather than a purely commercial one.
"It's a risk that we have been bearing almost entirely on our own, and it's something that needs to be internationalized or else we continue in a position -- the world continues in a position where up to 6 million barrels of refining capacity is not optimized," he said.
KPC has not amended its strategic development plans as a result of the conflict and is aiming to reach oil production capacity of 4 million b/d by 2035.
-- Rosemary Griffin

transiting the Strait of Hormuz, marking the fourth tanker attack this month, according to UK Maritime Trade Operations data through Oct. 4.
According to maritime security company Vanguard Tech, the ship that was hit is the Liberia-flagged tanker Lipsi, with all crew safe and no environmental damage known. Tanker manager Dynacom Tankers Management did not respond to a request for comment Oct. 5.
UKMTO did not provide a more specific location for the latest ship attack in its Oct. 4 report on X. The incident followed an Oct. 2 attack involving a crude oil tanker that was struck by an unknown projectile on its port side while sailing four nautical miles east of Oman, UKMTO said Oct. 3 on X.
Both ships reported that their crews were safe and that there was no environmental damage. UKMTO did not provide the vessels' names.
There have been 110 ship incidents related to the Middle East conflict and six ship attacks in the Red Sea this year, UKMTO said in an Oct. 2 report.
"Vessel traffic through the Strait of Hormuz remains approximately 75% below pre-conflict levels, with tankers continuing to dominate observed movements," it said in the report published on its website.
Automatic identification system satellites indicated that most ships are traveling along the northern Iranian route rather than closer to Oman at the southern end of the Strait of Hormuz, UKMTO said.
A military-based satellite system showed that observed traffic in the Strait of Hormuz is at a ratio of about 5-to-1, meaning five ships are not broadcasting their locations for every ship that is, according to the report.
The conclusion is that "a substantial proportion of vessels using the southern route are not broadcasting AIS or are otherwise not captured by AIS analysis," UKMTO said.
Over on the Red Sea, Marshall Islands-flagged tanker Chrystal Sky reported seeing multiple explosions about 60 nautical miles south of Mokha, Yemen, at 1650 GMT on Oct. 4, Vanguard Tech reported Oct. 4. One explosion occurred in the air within about 100 meters of the ship's starboard side, but it was not damaged and continued on its way, Vanguard said. Ship manager Executive Ship Management DMCC did not respond to a request for comment outside business hours.

WAF TRACKING: September crude loadings rise to 3.933 mil b/d: CAS
Nigerian loadings reach 1.801 mil b/d in Sep
Bonny Light largest volume grade across region at 318,000 b/d
China, India largest international destinations of WAF Sep loadings
Average crude oil loadings from West Africa rose to 3.933 million barrels/day in September, up from 3.85 million b/d in

August, according to data from S&P Global Commodities at Sea Oct. 5.
Nigeria held top spot with loadings of 1.801 million b/d, above the region's second-largest producer, Angola, with 1.123 million b/d in September. The only month so far in 2026 to see West Africa breach the 4 million b/d threshold was June, at 4.093 million b/d, CAS showed.
The highest-volume Nigerian crude grade was listed as flagship Bonny Light, with average loadings hitting 318,000 b/d, up from August's figure of 291,000 b/d, and marking it as the largest export grade across all of West Africa. Nigeria's Forcados was second, while Republic of the Congo's Djeno was third, with 301,000 b/d and 288,000 b/d, respectively.
China and India are set to take first and second place, respectively, in terms of the largest international importers of September-loading WAF crude volumes, with a combined 1.084 million b/d, though, when accounting for both international and domestic flows, Nigeria took second place at 562,000 b/d, largely owing to the Dangote refinery.
Indeed, the Dangote figure was derived exclusively from Nigerian grades, despite the refinery having previously purchased WAF crude volumes from other producers such as Angola, Ghana and Cameroon.
Some 192,000 b/d of Bonny Light loaded in September was taken by the refinery, representing over 60% of the grade's loadings through the month. Other Nigerian grades that saw loadings in September for the plant included Qua Iboe and Amenam, CAS data showed Oct. 5.
Lifting schedules for West Africa's November-loading program have been widely circulated with the new trading cycle kicking into gear. Sentiment remained largely bearish for Angolan and Congolese crudes, while trading sources told Platts previously that their Nigerian counterparts were seeing pricing hold up better than expected.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, last assessed Nigeria's Bonny Light at a $6.60/b premium to benchmark Dated Brent on Oct. 2, down more than $1/b week over week, though still above the grade's summer trough of a 45 cents/b discount as assessed across July 14-17.
-- George Delaney, Oliwier Wronski

-- Yong ren Toh
Platts North Sea Crude Daily Market Analysis
North Sea prompt barrels clear as demand remains strong Medium-term prospects murky as traders await stock
release details

Surging freight rates across the Atlantic basin remained a key concern for traders, with rates between key suppliers and Europe reaching fresh heights and elevating values on a landed basis for arbitrage flows.
"The prompt is almost cleared," said a Europe-based North Sea crude trader, adding that spiking values for delivered grades into Europe had seen some refineries turn to short-haul North Sea grades on the prompt as an alternative.
The Platts Market on Close continued to see interest for prompt barrels, with bids on both a FOB and CIF Rotterdam basis seen for all six Dated Brent basket grades. Notably, Octoberarriving WTI Midland CIF Rotterdam saw outstanding bidding interest as high as $22/barrel premiums over Dated Brent, while Oseberg FOB Sture reached a $21.55/b premium.
However, medium-term supply prospects remained murky, as the market continued to digest a G7 announcement late Oct. 2 that it would enforce a four-month timeline for member states to release 100 million barrels of oil stocks they had previously committed to in March.
Combined with previous announcements that the US would release Strategic Petroleum Reserve crude stocks, some traders saw weaker fundamentals for the second half of October -- although "the first half remains supported by bidding activity," said a second trader.
With bearish sentiment emerging, participants had pegged values for November-loading Forties cargoes below $10/b premiums over Dated Brent during Oct. 5 afternoon trading, with a large backwardated structure expected across the differential as well.
In the sour segment, Norway's Johan Sverdrup continued on a downward trajectory as October-loading barrels continued to be offered in the Oct. 5 MOC session, left outstanding at an $11/b premium over Dated Brent.
However, the widening sweet/sour spreads within the North Sea had elicited some skepticism from market participants, with some pointing to demonstrated $10/b premiums across some laycans for North Sea basket grades over Johan Sverdrup as difficult to comprehend.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Natasha Tan

Petroecuador declares force majeure on oil shipments from SOTE pipeline
SOTE pipeline to restart in six days
Pumping station fire is under investigation
SOTE shut twice in 2025
Petroecuador declared force majeure on crude shipments after halting pumping operations at its 360,000 b/d SOTE oil pipeline due to a fire at a pumping station.
A team of workers is carrying out repairs and aims to restart operations within the next six days, the state company said in a statement on Oct. 3.
CEO Sebastian Maag inspected ongoing repair work at the No. 4 Baeza pumping station, while an investigation is underway to ascertain the cause of the fire, Petroecuador added. The 498 km SOTE conduit, which links the company's Lago Agrio oilfields in the eastern jungle Oriente Basin with its 110,000 b/d Esmeraldas oil refinery on the northwestern Pacific coast, was halted twice last year
The SOTE fire adds to a slew of problems at Petroecuador's ageing installations, including shutdowns of the Esmeraldas refinery, the main processing plant at its Block 43 ITT oilfields and the 74,400 b/d Esmeraldas-Santo Domingo fuel pipeline earlier this year.
President Daniel Noboa, a 38-year-old businessman who was reelected to a second mandate last year, launched a $42 billion, five-year investment plan to boost oil production, including awarding oil and natural gas fields, refineries and pipelines as private concessions. Ecuador was averaging 460,000 b/d through the end of September, according to the Energy Ministry, but no figures were available for October. Petroecuador accounts for about 80% of the country's output.
-- Alex Emery

VENEZUELA DATA: Crude output edges up to 1.218 million b/d in September
PDVSA production rises 5,000 b/d on month
Orinoco Belt output climbs to 690,000 b/d
Power outages hamper infrastructure upgrades
Average oil production by Venezuelan state-owned PDVSA and its foreign partners rose slightly in September to 1.218 million barrels/day, from 1.213 million b/d in August, according to preliminary data from the Ministry of Hydrocarbons, reviewed by Platts, a part of S&P Global Energy.
Venezuelan production remained stable, though it was affected by prolonged power outages, especially in the west, as well as delays in upgrading the infrastructure supporting oil operations, according to the report.
The Ministry of Hydrocarbons report contains preliminary, unofficial information that can change after consolidation. The preliminary output data covers Productive Production Contracts (CPPHs) but does not include condensate production, which is estimated at 66,000 b/d for August, according to the report.
Western field The output from western fields, Zulia and Trujillo states,
remained at 359,000 b/d in September, similar to the August average.
The preliminary report showed that production at the fields operated by PDVSA in the west remained at an average of 70,000 b/d. Production from joint ventures and CPPH remained at an average of 288,000 b/d in September.
Production at Petroboscan (PDVSA 60%, Chevron 40%) fell to 115,307 b/d in September from 116, 757 b/d in August.
At the end of August, the US Departments of State and Defense reached a deal with North American Blue Energy Partners (NABEP) to expand oil production in Venezuela. As part of the deal, NABEP will have operating control of 17 fields, including five fields in Zulia state that were already under NABEP's control at Petrozamora.
Petrozamora's production rose to 98,369 b/d in September, up from the 97,816 b/d recorded in August. Production at seven other fields in Zulia state awarded to NABEP (Lagunillas Lago, Intercampo Norte, Tia Juana Lago, Lagomar, Rosa Medio, Lago Medio, and Lago 5) totaled 51,493 b/d in September, down from the August average of 53,626 b/d.
Until August, the Lagunillas Lago and Lago 5 fields were operated by China Concord Resources under a CPPH signed with the Venezuelan government in 2024. Meanwhile, the Intercampo Norte field was operated by the Sinovenezolana joint venture, according to previous reports.
In September, the production in other oil fields in Zulia state was as follows: Petroregional del Lago (PDVSA 60%, Maurel & Prom 40%), 22,131 b/d; and Petroquiriquire Occidente (PDVSA 60%, Spanish company Repsol 40%), 35,750 b/d.

In fields located in the states of Barinas and Apure, operated by the Colombian company Colven under a productive production contract, production was 10,000 b/d in September.
PDVSA's Production and Participation Contracts (CPP), now known as Primary Hydrocarbon Activity Contracts, were incorporated as a business model in the reform of the Hydrocarbons Law approved at the end of January.
Orinoco Belt Production in the Orinoco Belt, Venezuela's main oil-producing
area, climbed to 690,000 b/d in September, up 5,000 b/d from August, according to preliminary data.
The Orinoco Belt is divided into four main blocks: Carabobo, Ayacucho, Junín, and Boyacá. Production in the Boyacá block was zero in January-September.
In the Orinoco Belt, the average production of the joint venture Petropiar was 120,000 b/d in September, according to the report.
The U.S.-based company Chevron will schedule a major maintenance on the Petropiar upgrading plant, located in the José Antonio Anzoátegui Industrial Complex in Anzoátegui state, in eastern Venezuela, according to a previous report at Platts.
Petropiar upgrader has the capacity to process 190,000 b/d of API (American Petroleum Institute) 8 crude oil and produce the crude known as Hamaca Blend (API 26), which is exported and also processed at the Amuay refinery in Paraguaná, Falcón state. It can also operate in blending mode to produce Merey crude. In April, PDVSA and Chevron signed an agreement to integrate the Ayacucho 8 block into the operations of the joint venture Petropiar.
Additional details were not available.
East fields and offshore The average production from fields located in Monagas
state remained at 140,000 b/d in September, according to preliminary data.
Monagas state produces the Mesa and Santa Barbara light crudes that PDVSA uses in its refining system and also to dilute extra-heavy crudes from the Orinoco Belt.
Offshore production was 14,100 b/d and the production of PDVSA GAS was 4,900 b/d in September, according to the preliminary data.
In August, Venezuela reported average OPEC production of 1.201 million b/d, while secondary sources estimated production at 1.145 million b/d, according to the Monthly Oil Market Report.
Orinoco Belt PDVSA expects to reach crude oil production of 1.37 million
b/d by December, an increase of 430,000 b/d from the 940,000 b/d recorded in January, said the company's executive vice president, Jovanny Martinez, in a presentation to oil industry executives in Caracas on April 27.

ConocoPhillips sees US crude production as high as 14.5 mil b/d if high prices hold
Planning based on higher price floor of $70/b
Market needs more conventional oil: Lance
Syria gas output could ramp up from 2027
US oil production could rise to 14.5 million barrels per day if current price levels persist, ConocoPhillips Executive Chairman Ryan Lance said Oct. 5, while arguing that the global oil market has entered a structurally higher-price environment amid concerns over future conventional supply.
International crude prices have surged since the US-Iran war broke out at the end of February, underpinned by lower supplies from Middle Eastern producers as Red Sea and Hormuz shipping operations are disrupted.
"Our in-house view probably sees the US exceeding 14 million, maybe going to 14.5 million barrels a day if the prices stay where they're at today to accommodate the supply in the global market," Lance said at the Energy Intelligence Forum in London.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed Dated Brent at $126.955/b on Oct. 2, up 79% from $70.94/b on Feb. 27. WTI Midland was at $90.91/b, up 37.1%.
Lance said the character of US supply growth was changing, with a greater share of incremental liquids production coming from natural gas liquids rather than crude oil itself.
In the last couple of years, only 200,000-400,000 b/d of growth came from "black oil," with another 300,000-400,000 b/d coming from NGLs associated with rising gas production, according to his estimates.
"You haven't seen the rig count increase. What's driving this is the technology and efficiency of the underlying system," Lance said.
The comments are consistent with ConocoPhillips' long-held view that US shale production is moving toward a plateau rather than another phase of breakneck expansion. S&P Global Energy CERA expects US crude production to increase from 13.7 million b/d in 2025 to 13.8 million b/d in 2026 before a further hike to 14.1 million b/d in 2027.
Price floor On the longer-term outlook, Lance said the industry's price
floor had risen, though few market participants expect oil prices near $90/b to persist indefinitely.
Producers increasingly view about $70/b as a realistic downside level, with ConocoPhillips' internal planning assumptions based on a midcycle environment of roughly $65/b WTI and $70/b Brent, he said.
The big strategic question for the industry, he added, is where future conventional oil supply will come from as unconventional production in North America matures.
He linked renewed exploration spending, merger and acquisition activity, and continued industry consolidation to expectations that global oil demand growth will eventually

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

10

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 5, 2026

require new conventional supply sources. The International Energy Agency currently expects global oil
consumption to fall by 2.5 million b/d this year before recovering by 2.6 million b/d in 2027, when oil flows from the Middle East could return to the pre-war levels.
China has significantly cut crude imports this year amid a high oil price environment, but Lance said recent import data suggest demand is beginning to recover. China would remain a major crude importer over the long term because domestic resources are insufficient to support economic growth, he added.
Syrian gas In June, ConocoPhillips signed a deal with state-owned Syrian
Petroleum Co. and Novaterra Energy to develop multiple natural gas fields and boost production from several existing sites in the country.
Lance said the company is working with the Syrian government and a partner to revive mature gas fields and restore energy infrastructure damaged during years of conflict.
Domestically produced gas could be developed at an equivalent cost of less than $7-$8 per million British thermal units, compared with LNG imports that may cost Syria $14-$15/ MMBtu, according to the CEO.
"There's a fiscal incentive for the country to do that, and they've given us access to the land, to the resource, and we got to put in some equipment that is moving quickly to start producing," he said, adding that gas output could be ramped up next year.
The company is also discussing opportunities to expand beyond gas into oil developments and is evaluating offshore exploration potential, Lance said.
On Venezuela, Lance reiterated skepticism about the country's investment framework despite recent reforms.
He said the current hydrocarbons law effectively leaves the government with an up to 95% share of oil income, making it difficult to attract significant international capital.
Lance said Venezuela also faces a substantial debt burden and must resolve outstanding financial obligations. ConocoPhillips remains the largest non-sovereign creditor to the country following the expropriation of its assets in 2007-08, and he indicated the company is focused on resolving those claims rather than "putting a bunch of billion dollars" in new investment.
-- Max Lin

ICE front-month Singapore gasoline swap open interest rises 8.15% MOM in Sept
Singapore 92 RON gasoline swaps open interest up 8.15% MOM in Sept
Chinese exports expected to plunge in Oct
Total open interest in front-month October Singapore gasoline derivative contracts traded on the Intercontinental Exchange

rose 7.49% month over month in September, to 28.244 million barrels, the latest ICE data showed Oct. 4.
Open interest for the front-month October Singapore 92 RON gasoline swap contract rose 8.15% month over month to 17.658 million barrels in September.
Near-term market liquidity improved amid expectations of a sharp drop in China's oil product exports during the Oct. 1-7 National Day Golden Week holiday, Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, reported Oct. 1.
The late release of the third batch of export quotas left many exporting refiners with only a brief customs-clearance window on Sept. 30, four refining sources at Sinopec and PetroChina told Platts Oct. 1.
Platts assessed the front-month Singapore 92 RON gasoline time spread at an average of $9.64/b in September, nearly double the August average, indicating stronger sentiment across the gasoline complex during the month.
The FOB Singapore 92 RON gasoline cash differential averaged a premium of $8.76/b in September, widening from $4.22/b in August, Platts data showed.
US retail gasoline prices, often used as a proxy for demand, inched down to an average of $4.491/gal in September, compared to $4.192/gal in August, according to data from the US Energy Information Administration.
Blending demand was heard healthy and likely to extend into Q4 2026, according to a Southeast Asia-based analyst, mid low inventories.
Indonesia -- the region's largest importer of gasoline -- also saw its currency weakening slightly, which could also moderate demand for the product.
By the end of September, the Indonesian rupiah traded at 17,937 per US dollar, compared to 17,736 at the start of the month.
Indonesia's headline inflation accelerated to 3.28% year over year in September, while prices rose 0.30% month over month, the latest data from Statistics Indonesia showed. The annual inflation rate was close to the market expectation of a 3.30% increase and was higher than August's 3.19% reading.
Indonesian demand was heard healthy across the month, according to sources, where Pertamina sought Indonesia sought 9.1 million barrels of 90 RON gasoline for November-December loading via a tender that closed Sept. 24, and 1.3 million barrels of 92 RON gasoline for the same period via a tender that closed Sept. 24.
Meanwhile, open interest for the front-month Singapore 92 RON gasoline swap against ICE Brent futures crack rose 6.40% month over month to 10.586 million barrels.
The Platts-assessed front-month FOB Singapore 92 RON gasoline swap crack spread against front-month Brent swap averaged $27.57/b in September, widening 44.41% from $19.09/b in August, Platts data showed.
Mid-term view firmed month over month. Platts assessed the Q4/Q1 Asian 92 RON gasoline swaps time spread at an average of plus $13.09/b over September, widening from an average spread of plus $5.12/b the previous month.

Platts European Gasoline Daily Market Analysis
Barge differentials highly volatile on day
Repar refinery shuts for maintenance
Gasoline markets were volatile Oct. 5, with cash differentials moving over $20/metric ton in the barge market before the close, while paper markets softened.
E5 barges opened the day trading below levels heard Oct. 2, at $120/mt versus November Eurobob, before climbing to trade at $140/mt later in the day. E10 similarly jumped from $105/mt to $130/mt through the day.
However, by the close, paper markets had softened, with the November EBOB crack spread dropping $1.42/b to $30.63/b, and the Mo1/Mo2 spread dropping almost $5/mt.
Markets were volatile across all oil products on the day. The Platts Market on Close was relatively quiet, however, with one barge trade. Trafigura bid for EBOB barges, which traded at competitive levels.
In the Mediterranean, Platts assessed the FOB Med gasoline price at a $73.25/mt cash differential to balance-of-month October swaps, down from $79.25/mt the day prior. Differentials against balmo October fell over $50/mt last week on very competitive offers from BGN and Hellenic in the MOC.
In refinery news, Brazil's Petrobras will start a maintenance shutdown at the Repar refinery in Parana state Oct. 5, part of $150 million in investments to modernize and expand the 207,000 barrels/day facility that supplies refined products for the country's southern region, according to the company.
The refinery supplies about 85% of demand in the Parana, Santa Catarina and Mato Grosso do Sul states, as well as the southern portion of Sao Paulo state.
Platts assessed the front-month FOB AR Eurobob barge at $1,077.50/mt, up $4.75/mt on the day, while the equivalent crack was assessed at $30.63/b, down $1.42/b from the previous session.
The October-November spread was unchanged at $94/mt, while the November-December spread was down $4.75/mt at $70.25/mt.
The October Mediterranean-Northwest Europe gasoline differential, the spread between October FOB Mediterranean swaps and the equivalent FOB ARA Eurobob barge, was assessed at a $20/mt premium, up $1/mt on the day. The November differential was down 50 cents/mt at a $17.50/mt premium.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Matthew Tracey-cook

Total open interest for all front-month October Singapore jet fuel contracts -- including jet/kerosene and jet kerosene/gasoil 10 parts per million sulfur -- on the Intercontinental Exchange fell 28.54% month over month to 18.787 million barrels in September, the latest ICE data showed Oct. 2.
Open interest for front-month October jet fuel/kerosene contracts fell 33.13% month over month to 7.433 million barrels over the same period, the data showed.
A fall in open interest tracked a well-supplied region, where steady outflows from North Asia towards the end of September eased the Asian jet fuel/kerosene complex.
Still, in the first half of September, regional aviation fuel supply saw some increased pressure as refiners were heard maximizing gasoil production due to a weak regrade.
The Platts-assessed front-month regrade swap -- the value jet fuel commands over 10 ppm sulfur gasoil -- widened to an average of minus $3.09/barrel in September, from minus $2.96/b in August, and minus $1.53/b in July. Platts is part of S&P Global Energy.
Open interest for the front-month October Singapore regrade fell 23.71% month over month to 11.038 million barrels in September, the data showed.
Open interest for the front-month October Singapore jet fuel/kerosene against the front-month Dubai swap crack fell 55.24% month over month to 316,000 barrels in September.
The Platts-assessed FOB Singapore jet fuel/kerosene swap crack spread against front month Dubai swaps -- a measure of the product's relative strength to the crude it was refined from -- averaged $71.30/b in September, up 10.62% from August's average at $64.45/b, and up 18.32% from July's average at $60.26/b.
Moving forward, the Asian jet fuel/kerosene complex may see some tightening as some Chinese cargoes set to load in October for export to destinations outside of Hong Kong and Macau were heard to have been canceled, according to sources with direct knowledge of the matter.

Diesel
Platts European Diesel Daily Market Analysis
Gasoil futures trade below $1,400/mt
High prices suppress European diesel demand
The European diesel complex remained relatively subdued on Oct. 5, with gasoil futures trading below the $1,400/metric ton mark, following news that the G7 would release 100 million barrels of oil stocks in the coming months.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the front-month ICE low sulfur gasoil futures (LSGO) contract at $1,383.50/mt Oct. 5, up $28, but still well below the September average of $1,465.25/mt.
Physical flat prices saw similar movements day over day, with 10 parts per million diesel CIF Northwest Europe cargoes rising $24.25 to $1,399.25/mt and Mediterranean cargoes pushing up $26.75 to $1,397.75/mt.
The relative weakness comes as the market anticipates increased flows and softening fundamentals following the potential release of oil stocks from G7 countries.
"It's quite a significant volume that would be available," said one European diesel trader. "Other releases have been slow to reach the market ... if they did it quicker this time, it could have a significant impact."
"It does look like a [temporary] fix rather than a real solution," said a trader in the Mediterranean region, reflecting some of the pessimism in the market about long-term improvements in supply. "Let's see how things go while Russia and Ukraine continue fighting."
Falling flat prices could see an uptick in demand, which would, in turn, reduce product availability across Europe.
The first trader said that "demand is particularly weak with high flat prices," and the latest news could "stimulate some demand."
"Overall, there is an overhang of product," said another market participant based in Europe. "[As barrels [are] available in the Med, people don't want to meet the commitment with the backwardation where it is.
"Prices are too high ... with a lack of actual demand," the market participant added.
-- Sophia Aung

Marine Fuel
VLSFO crack weakens to six-month low amid tepid demand
NW Europe VLSFO crack hits six-month low
Higher production meets softening demand
Closed arbitrage limits export opportunities
The front-month Northwest European very low sulfur fuel oil paper barge crack reached a six-month low, pressured by sufficient supply and muted demand.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the front-month marine fuel 0.5%S FOB Rotterdam barge swap against the equivalent Brent swap crack at minus $1.879/b on Oct. 2, down $2.17/b from the previous session.
This marked the lowest level since March 31, when the swap was assessed at minus $3.203 cents/b, according to Platts data, reflecting a six-month low.
All prompt-month VLSFO paper barge cracks retreated on Oct. 2.
In the physical market, higher production and subdued demand have contributed to a falling VLSFO crack environment over September and into October.
On the supply side, lower feedstock prices have seen higher volumes of product being blended into the VLSFO bunkering pool, as blending economics have improved.
Market participants note that across Northwest Europe, several sellers are offering LSSR product at "discounts" to brent, which can go into the fuel oil pool.
Demand, meanwhile, has softened, with minimal volumes leaving Europe for the Americas and Singapore amid elevated freight costs.
"Arbs probably the most closed they've ever been," said a European-focused trader source.
While local bunkering demand faces the headwind of higher prices, shipowners were reluctant to buy more VLSFO volumes than necessary, as they wait for lower prices.
-- Joseph Jaffe, Tommy Petrou

Fujairah Oct ex-wharf bunker premiums rise on supply delays: sources

Fujairah's bunker market has tightened as persistent supply disruptions and cargo arrival delays pushed ex-wharf premiums higher for October supply, five trading sources said.

to the benchmark Platts FOB Singapore marine fuel 0.5% sulfur cargo values, firming from the $70-$80/mt range initially seen for September supply, according to trader sources.

Platts European Naphtha Daily Market Analysis
Naphtha crack rises 50 cents amid tightness
Rhine levels at 9 cm restrict barge flows
The European naphtha front-month crack edged higher on Oct. 5, while prompt backwardation narrowed marginally.
The European naphtha market remained supported by tight prompt availability, with recent inventory draws and ongoing

logistical constraints helping to sustain a firm backwardated structure, even with curtailed gasoline blending demand.
Northwest European fundamentals continued to reflect a split market, with supply-side tightness providing support following last week's Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp stock draws, while downstream demand signals remained softer.
Low Rhine water levels remained a key constraint on the Northwest European market, restricting barge movements from coastal terminals to inland demand centers and limiting flexibility for both naphtha and finished-product flows. Water levels at Kaub showed little improvement, remaining critically low at 9 centimeters on Oct. 5, with forecasts indicating only limited improvement in the coming days, according to German water authority WSV data.
At the same time, naphtha demand from gasoline blenders has continued to ease as the market moves further away from peak summer specification demand. Elevated flat prices have also weighed on buying appetite among petrochemical end users, while tightness in high-octane components has reduced the scope for blenders to absorb larger volumes of lower-octane naphtha.
The high-octane complex remained firm, with MTBE prices trading at elevated levels. Limited import arrivals, reduced European production rates, and barge constraints have kept octane supply tight, limiting blending flexibility across Northwest Europe.
Broader clean tanker tightness continued to reinforce the freight headwind, keeping long-haul export economics difficult and limiting the pull on Northwest European gasoline barrels.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the physical naphtha CIF NWE cargo price at $872/metric ton, up $19.75/mt day ov day.
Platts assessed the front-month CIF NWE naphtha crack at minus $5.25/barrel, up 50 cents/b day over day, with the equivalent swap up $21/mt at $831.75/mt.
Platts assessed the October/November time spread at $30/mt, down 50 cents/mt day over day, while the November/ December spread was down $1/mt at $27.50/mt.
-- Harry Deighton

Platts European Propane Daily Commentary
Tight supply leaves October demand unmet
Rhine restrictions strain transport options
CIF cargo prices jump $35.75/mt daily
Northwest European propane availability remained tight on Oct. 5, as outstanding second-half October buying requirements and persistent Rhine restrictions kept attention on cargo supply and inland logistics.
Some demand had shifted into early November, but other buyers continued to seek volumes for late October, with limited availability to meet those requirements.
"I still see some decent pockets of late October, second-half October demand unmet," one market source said.
The source added that there was "not a whole lot of volume around" to cover outstanding demand.
The Rhine remained a primary concern, with conditions showing little sign of improvement and continuing to disrupt inland deliveries.

Restricted barge capacity increased reliance on trucks and rail, placing alternative transport options under growing strain.
Challenging transatlantic arbitrage economics and competition from Asian destinations continued to constrain prospects for additional imports, limiting replacement supply options.
Elevated TTF prices supported propane consumption as refinery fuel.
However, a narrowing discount to naphtha reduced propane's competitive advantage for flexible crackers, bringing relative feedstock costs further into focus.
Outstanding cargo requirements and logistical constraints nevertheless continued to underpin the market.
In the CIF propane cargo market, two bids were heard in the Platts Market on Close assessment process, one from BASF and one from Gunvor SA.
One of these bids was deemed competitive and set the assessment.
Gunvor bid a TOT23 cargo, CIF Flushing, for Oct. 20-24 delivery, at a 50% $785/metric ton flat price and a 50% CIF AmsterdamRotterdam-Antwerp balance-month next-day plus $25/mt.
Platts assessed the CIF large cargo market in outright terms at $782.75/mt, up $35.75/mt day over day, at a $24.75/mt premium to the propane CIF October swap, a $4.75/mt increase in premium levels.
In the propane coaster market, no bids, offers, or trades were heard in the MOC. In the absence of competitive indications, the market was assessed on trader sentiment.
Platts assessed the propane coaster market at $818/mt, up $41/mt day over day, at a $35.25/mt premium to large cargoes.
Platts assessed the FOB ARA barges up 25 cents/mt day over day at plus $35.25/mt versus the CIF large propane market, while FCA ARA market premiums to CIF large were assessed at plus $48.25/mt.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Baran Serdaroglu
Tankers
Japan sanctions tankers tied to 42.7 mil barrels of Russian oil trade
Japan counters shadow fleet, joining other G7 members
Vessels mainly transport Russian oil to Asia
Asset freezes target 33 Russian entities
Tankers targeted in Japan's first sanctions against the shadow fleet have been actively involved in international oil trades, according to cargo-tracking data, as the G7 continues to tighten scrutiny over ships transporting Russian energy.
On Oct. 2, Japan's foreign ministry announced sanctions on four MR, two LR1, five Suezmax and 20 Aframax tankers as well as two general cargo ships and two roll-on/roll-off ships, joining other G7 members in targeting Russia's seaborne trades over the war in Ukraine.

October 5, 2026

The tankers have lifted nearly 42.7 million barrels of crude and petroleum products this year, nearly all of them from Russia for shipments to India, China, Southeast Asia and other destinations, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea.
S&P Global Maritime Intelligence Risk Suite data shows the ships were sanctioned by at least one other Western authority before Japan's enforcement, which didn't cover LNG carriers. Japan remains a Russian LNG buyer, mainly from the Sakhalin-2 project.
Western governments had sanctioned a total of 1,226 oil tankers and LPG carriers as of Aug. 31, according to S&P Global Energy Horizons data, and those ships were mainly designated due to their links to Iranian or Russian trades.
Japan's measures impose restrictions on specified service transactions involving the vessels, as well as certain capital transactions related to their sale, purchase, lease or charter, according to the foreign ministry.
The sanctions package also includes asset freezes on 33 Russian entities and nine individuals, export restrictions on four companies based in Turkey and the UAE, and additional controls on goods that could contribute to Russia's industrial capabilities.
The sanctions were introduced "in view of the current international situation over Ukraine, and for the purpose of contributing to the international efforts for achieving international peace aimed at a solution of the issue, in line with the measures taken by other major countries," the ministry said on its website.

Platts East of Suez Dirty Tanker Daily Commentary
VLCC rates surge as owners regain control
Western strength expected to lift eastern Suezmax market
Aframax owners push higher amid tight tonnage
The East of Suez VLCC market moved sharply higher on Oct. 5, with market fundamentals increasingly favoring owners, sources said. "The West market is catching up with Asia. West Africa was lagging for some time. There are plenty of ships, but many are controlled by Sinokor," a VLCC broker said.
"Last week, a lot of Suezmax cargoes were covered and the Suezmax market has gone crazy, which has strengthened VLCC owners' resolve as well. Rates are climbing and owners are likely to control the market today," another VLCC broker said, echoing that view.
Among fixtures heard after the market close, Admic was reported to have placed the Dubai Energy on subjects for an Oct. 21 loading from Fujairah to North Asia at w780, with a Southeast Asia discharge option at w830.
Elsewhere, SK and Hyundai Oilbank were heard to have taken the DHT Gazelle and an unnamed VLCC on subjects for Oct. 16-17 loadings from Yanbu to South Korea at lump-sum rates of $55 million each.

The voyages were expected to transit both the Suez Canal and the Cape of Good Hope instead of the Bab al-Mandab Strait.
The Suezmax market also strengthened, supported by surging rates in the Atlantic Basin.
A Suezmax broker said it would be hard to maintain the current level and that the market would move higher.
Several brokers echoed this view, saying strength in the West African market was likely to push Persian Gulf Suezmax freight higher during the week, despite limited loading demand on Oct. 5.
Among reported fixtures, IOC was heard to have taken a Dynacom Suezmax on subjects for an Oct. 21 loading from Basrah to Vadinar at a lump-sum rate of $22 million.
Market sources said the freight level included all voyagerelated costs, waiting time and AWRP, all for the owner's account.
The Aframax market was also firmer, with owners continuing to show strong confidence amid a relatively tight tonnage list.
"Owners were quoting much higher levels for northbound voyages. There are also a lot of TMX, PTP and Argentina cargoes being rolled over. Owners are more interested in long-haul business than local cargoes," an Aframax owner said.
"It will continue to climb. I saw the latest Suezmax fixtures in the West as well, so it looks like every vessel sector is trending higher," an Aframax broker said.
"Tonnage availability in the East remains relatively tight and owners are still very bullish," the same source added.
On the cargo side, Trafigura was heard seeking an Aframax for an Oct. 17 loading from Singapore to Zhoushan, while PVTrans was also in the market for an Aframax for a cross-Vietnam voyage loading around Oct. 19.

News
South Korea sustains ample APAC clean product exports on stable feedstock supply
Refiners fully secure Oct. crude needs: MOTIR
Diesel exports to Australia jump 18% YTD
Aug clean oil product exports to Europe reach record high
Gasoil crack spreads hit record $83/barrel
South Korea's refining industry expects stable crude procurement through year-end, supported by government measures, enabling major refiners to sustain clean oil product exports to Asia-Pacific customers and help cover essential fuel shortfalls across the region, according to refinery sources, Korea Petroleum Association, and the trade ministry over Sept.29-Oct 5.
South Korea, Asia's top clean oil products supplier and net exporter, has fully secured its feedstock crude needs for October, while crude oil arrivals for the rest of the fourth quarter are expected to reach at least 90% of the previous year's monthly import levels, according to feedstock managers at major refiners based in Ulsan, Seoul and Yeosu, as well as the Ministry of Trade, Industry and Resources in its industry briefing presentation slide for October.

With about 3.4 million barrels/day of refining capacity and domestic oil demand of around 2.3 million b/d, South Korea has about 1 million b/d of surplus refining capacity available for exports, according to data from state-run Korea National Oil Corp. and KPA.
As the global imbalance between clean oil products supply and demand has intensified, South Korea's refining industry has been helping to address diesel supply shortages in various economic partner countries, responding to requests from countries such as Australia and New Zealand, KPA said in its industry report Oct. 2.
In the first eight months, South Korea exported 62.03 million barrels of refined oil products, mostly diesel and gasoline, to Australia, up 18% from the same period a year earlier, the latest data from state-run Korea National Oil Corp. issued Sept. 25 showed.

Through rigorous diversification efforts, plenty of light sweet US crude, as well as heavy sour Canadian and various Latin American grades, are poised to regularly arrive, while 1.1 million b/d of staple Middle Eastern sour crudes have also been secured for the rest of the year, a MOTIR official told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy on Sept. 30.
Backed up by stability in feedstock procurement and inflows, South Korean refiners can focus on fulfilling term-contract commitments for gasoline, diesel and jet fuel to Asia-Oceania customers, while also supplying spot cargoes to regional markets facing acute shortages, middle distillate product managers at major refiners based in Ulsan and Seoul, as well as marketing managers at two South Korean refiners' trading offices based in Singapore told Platts over market discussion sessions over Oct. 1-5.

Shipments of essential fuels and oil products over JanuaryAugust to New Zealand also rose 11% year over year to 16.7 million barrels, KNOC data showed.
Among recent spot market deals concluded in the Asian market, South Korea's second-biggest refiner GS Caltex sold 300,000 barrels of 50 parts per million sulfur gasoil for loading over Oct. 23-27 at a discount of about $1.50/b to the loadingmonth average of Mean of Platts Singapore 10 parts per million sulfur gasoil assessments, FOB.

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

19

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 5, 2026

Apart from the regular Asia-Pacific outlets, South Korea sold a record-high 5.73 million barrels of clean oil products, including gasoline, gasoil and jet fuel, to Europe in August, KNOC data showed.
Robust crack, lower Chinese exports South Korean refiners could find additional opportunities to
supply Asia-Pacific markets as steep cuts in Chinese exports tighten regional availability and support exceptionally strong middle distillate margins, the Singapore-based middle distillate marketers and the Seoul-based product manager said.
Market participants expect China's October exports to fall to about 2 million mt, sharply below the previous estimate of 3.85 million mt and estimated September shipments of 4.36 million mt, Platts reported previously.
The pullback has left regional buyers seeking alternative gasoline, diesel and jet fuel supplies. Australia could be particularly affected after a 35,000-mt ultra low sulfur diesel cargo and a similar-sized jet fuel shipment from China, both scheduled for mid-October loading, were withdrawn from shipping fixtures, according to a Singapore-based shipping source.
Tighter Chinese supply has strengthened regional refining margins. Platts assessed the second-month Singapore gasoil swap crack against Dubai crude swaps at a record high of $83.02/b on Sept. 16. The crack spread has averaged $72.97/b in September, up from $64.71/b in August, $56.14/b in July and the 2025 average of $18.16/b.

The elevated cracks offer South Korean refiners a strong incentive to maximize exports once domestic supply requirements and term commitments have been met, the Seoulbased refinery product manager told Platts.
With crude procurement stabilizing and China's export suspension potentially extending into November, South Korean refiners are well positioned to offer incremental spot cargoes to Australia, New Zealand and other Asian markets facing supply shortfalls, according to the Singapore-based middle distillate marketers and KPA.
Nevertheless, overseas sales will remain subject to government measures requiring refiners to prioritize domestic fuel security, with the export volumes capped at 2025 shipment levels.

Strait of Hormuz traffic picks up with nine VLCC transits on Oct. 4
Hormuz transits at 25 ships, most since Sept. 30
Middle East crude exports average 14.5 mil b/d in Sept
LNG carriers resurface after signal blackouts
Nine VLCCs transited the Strait of Hormuz on Oct. 4, the most since Sept. 22, while the Bab al-Mandab Strait had no VLCC crossings for the 10th consecutive day, S&P Global Commodities at Sea said in an Oct. 5 report.
Total traffic through Hormuz rose to 25 ships on Oct. 4 from 21 ships on Oct. 3 while Bab al-Mandab transits dropped to 31 ships from 35 ships over the same period, according to the CAS report. Hormuz traffic was the highest since Sept. 30 and has climbed every day this month from 14 ships on Oct. 1, CAS data show.
Two LNG tankers left the Persian Gulf in recent days: the Indonesia-flagged Gas Garuda on Oct. 2 and the Bahamasflagged Al Kharsaah on Oct. 1, according to CAS.
Five of the nine VLCCs transiting Hormuz were linked to Saudi Arabia, with four of them heading into the Persian Gulf, according to the report. The last Saudi-linked ship to use the Bab al-Mandab Strait was on July 31, according to the data.
Average crude oil exports from the Middle East, including shipments from Saudi Arabia on the Red Sea coast, were 14.5 million b/d in September, about 66% of the averages in January and February before the Iran war began in late February, according to CAS data. The seven-day moving average on Sept. 30 reached 16.2 million b/d, the data show.
Several LNG carriers that had previously operated without active location signals later reappeared outside the Strait of Hormuz: the QatarEnergy-operated tankers Al Sadd and Al Gattara resurfaced off India's west coast while the Bahamasflagged Al Kharsaah reappeared near Muscat, Oman, after crossing Hormuz on Oct. 1, CAS said. Chevron carries out repairs at Venezuela's Jose oil terminal
Chevron expects first crude export from TAEJ Jan-Feb Berths 4, 5, and 7 at Guaraguao terminal to be repaired Merey's storage facility will also be repaired
The US-based company Chevron began repairs on three of the six marine docks at the José Storage and Shipping Terminal (TAEJ), located in Puerto La Cruz, Anzoátegui state, which is owned by the Venezuelan state-owned company PDVSA, according to sources.
"We have begun repairs on berths 4, 5, and 7 at the Guaraguao terminal, including dredging the navigation channel," said a Chevron Venezuela manager at a meeting with workers held at the facility on Oct. 2, according to people who attended the meeting.
"We expect the first Chevron tanker to leave this port between January and February," said the Chevron manager, whose name was withheld by the source.
"The storage tanks will also be repaired so that the terminal can operate under optimal conditions," the Chevron manager said, according to the source.
The TAEJ, also known as Guaraguao terminal, is a PDVSA crude oil storage and transportation facility located in the José Antonio Anzoátegui Industrial Complex on Venezuela 's Caribbean coast.
The TAEJ has three platforms and six loading berths capable of servicing vessels up to VLCC size. Currently, this terminal has an average operational autonomy of 1.1 days. At least 1.8 million barrels of storage capacity for Merey crude and 1.0 million barrels for Mesa crude are needed to achieve an approximate autonomy of 5 days, according to a PDVSA official who confirmed the information but requested anonymity because he was not authorized to speak to the media.
"The purpose of the meeting with the workers was to dispel rumors and ensure that jobs would be preserved," the PDVSA official said.

"We need to increase dispatch capacity to at least 30 shipments per month to handle the expected increase in production by 2027," said the PDVSA official.
The amount of Chevron's investment in the TAEJ was not disclosed.
The reform of the Hydrocarbons Law approved in late January allows international oil companies to operate PDVSA facilities, which remain the property of the Venezuelan state-owned company.
The reforms to the oil law were carried out just a few weeks after the capture in Caracas of former Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro by US forces.
The US Office of Foreign Assets Control (OFAC) issued updated specific and general licenses, such as Licenses 46E, 48D, and 49B, which allow companies such as Chevron, among others, to perform asset maintenance, transport, and export crude oil.
The terms of the agreement between PDVSA and Chevron to perform maintenance on the Guaraguao terminal were not available.
Neither PDVSA nor Chevron was immediately available for comment.
Chevron has entered into several agreements and contracts with PDVSA. In September, the two companies signed agreements for operational expansion and financial investment commitments within the Orinoco Oil Belt.
In April, Chevron signed an agreement with PDVSA to increase production at PetroPiar, as well as to raise its stake to 49% in Petroindependencia and secure exploration rights in the Ayacucho 8 block for PetroPiar.
In late 2022, Chevron and PDVSA signed contracts to extend the term of operations at the companies Petroboscán, Petropiar, and Petroindependencia.
Chevron's production in Venezuela totaled 280,000 b/d in September, according to the preliminary report from the Ministry of Hydrocarbons. This volume represents 23% of Venezuela's total production, which averaged 1,218 million b/d in September, according to the report.
Infrastructure that is obsolete and has not received maintenance for several years has been identified as a limiting factor to increasing oil production in Venezuela by companies partnering with PDVSA in joint ventures, as well as by those that have signed hydrocarbon production contracts (CPPH) in recent months, according to previous reports.
-- Mery Mogollon
Long-term energy demand drives ExxonMobil investment over policy: executive
Exxon prioritizes cost over policy shifts
Global energy needs to rise 15% by 2050
Permian and Guyana drive production growth
ExxonMobil's investment strategy remains focused on longterm global energy demand and project economics rather than

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

21

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 5, 2026

shifts in government policy, a senior company executive said Oct. 5, as the industry navigates changing US energy priorities.
"We're supplying products that society absolutely needs and we'll need 15% more of the total energy by 2050," ExxonMobil Vice President of Global Exploration John Ardill said at the Energy Intelligence Forum in London.
The comments come after years of sharply different approaches toward the oil and gas industry under successive US administrations, ranging from the Biden administration's emphasis on decarbonization and clean-energy incentives to US President Donald Trump's support for increased domestic fossil fuel production.
Ardill said ExxonMobil evaluates projects using investment horizons measured in decades rather than electoral cycles. "The bigger picture is really not that impacted by ... a few years' cycle," he said.
Instead, the US major's capital allocation decisions are guided by a project's ability to deliver low-cost supply. "We're not building capacity and growing production at any cost," Ardill said. "The capital allocation prioritization absolutely is based on what's the delivery cost of the molecules."
The executive highlighted ExxonMobil's annual capital investment of about $25 billion/year in the Permian Basin and offshore Guyana, which has been its driver for production growth in recent years.
Production from the Permian Basin and Guyana in the second quarter averaged over 1.8 million barrels per day of oil equivalent and over 900,000 b/d, respectively, according to ExxonMobil's last quarterly update.
The company's total upstream production reached 4.55 million boe/d in the first half of 2026, compared with 4.3 million boe/d for the whole of 2024. ExxonMobil has targeted raising output to 5.4 million boe/d by 2030, including 2.5 million boe/d from the Permian.
"The big opportunity in the future is to try and realize higher recoveries" in the Permian shale output, Ardill said, adding that more than 90% of hydrocarbons in place remain in the ground.
Meanwhile, the executive confirmed Guyana's installed production capacity is expected to exceed 1 million b/d by the end of 2026, up from 900,000 b/d currently.
The remarks came as an ExxonMobil floating production storage and offloading vessel, Errea Wittu, connected to the Uaru field, is set to add roughly 250,000 b/d to the capacity. The company has aimed for a capacity of 1.3 million b/d by 2027 and 1.7 million b/d by 2030 in Guyana.
